ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e268	Honourable co-representatives! Former poacher and poacher President Jo Benkow is dead, 88 years old. With his passing away, an important voice in the social debate is silent. Benkow was a liberal and clear politician, who will not least be remembered for his commitment to tolerance, and against discrimination and prejudice. Benkow was born in Trondheim and grew up in Bærum. In 1942, the male members of the family fled to Sweden, where Benkow graduated in 1943. After that, officers were trained and assigned to the Air Force in Canada and the United Kingdom. After the war, Benkow completed his photographic education and established his own business. He quickly became involved in local politics – first as chairman of Østfold Young Right and later as a member of the local government and the chairmanship of Bærum. In 1965 he was elected as a permanent representative at Stortinget from Akershus. This was the start of a 28-year growth career, culminating in eight years as the Grand President from 1985 to 1993. With his background, he helped to expand the knowledge of the Big One and to strengthen the representativeness of the Big One. At Stortinget, Benkow was primarily a social politician. He was a hot defender of the welfare state. A spokesman for tolerance, understanding and respect for differences. In the role of President-in-Office Benkow contributed to strengthening the role and reputation of the Stortinge. He represented Stortinget in a worthy and good way. The big business traditions, custom and use, and improving the working conditions of representatives and employees, were important to Benkow. In the time at Stortinget he also held central positions as chairman of the Right, Parliamentary Head and Chairman of the Judicial Committee. Transatlantic, European and Nordic cooperation was central to Benkow. He marked himself as a Nordic, and was for a time president of the Nordic Council. With the book «From the Synagogue to the Lion's Hill» Jo Benkow's dramatic life story became an all-man's life story. It gave strength and authority to his struggle against intolerance and prejudice. He was – as it has been said – a ‘moral compass for and in Norwegian society’. We remember Jo Benkow for his work in the Storting and shine peace over his memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e408	As the rapporteur for the Social Security Communication, the Terrorism Alert Communication and the three other issues we are now dealing with at the same time, I would like to thank the Committee for its very good and effective cooperation. Where the possibility of consensus has been within reach, we have managed to bring the Committee together. There has also been good dialogue with the two parties that are not in the Committee, the Christian People's Party and the Left. In particular, the state of social security is characterised by broad consensus on most issues. That's good. No one has committed to political games but has contributed to the widest possible agreed national basis for social security work. Our task, as a magnificence and government, is to lay the foundations for an even safer Norway, with all of that involved, and I believe that we do that together. At the same time, we decide today to allocate nearly 90 million. extra to the police's response to terrorism. In the position of preparedness for terrorism, an overall committee is committed to the objectives of the government’s strategy to prevent and deal with terrorism, a strategy that will also contribute to strengthening efforts in the fight against related, serious crime. The objectives are: preventing radicalisation and violent extremism to cooperate internationally on the prevention and combating of terrorism to avert and detect acts of terrorism before they take place to protect society and to make it well equipped against terrorist attacks to deal with terrorist attacks in the best possible way – whether it would happen to us in some way or not. The Committee stresses that the implementation of these objectives – and the related actions – must respect our democratic values, the principles of the rule of law, privacy and the security of citizens and nations in a balanced manner. The Committee points out that the July 22 Commission was clear in its report, one of the main conclusions being: Too much went wrong — and important parts of the authorities’ preparedness and capacity for crisis management were not good enough. The Committee recognises the Commission's report as a sound basis for decision-making actions to improve social security. With a few very few exceptions, a Joint Committee joins in the various measures taken by the Government for: preventing past and improved international cooperation to detect and avert terrorism in order to strengthen protection, in particular of critical infrastructure and societal critical functions, so as to reduce vulnerability to an acceptable level; The Committee further adheres to the many measures described in the Communication in order to enhance the ability to deal with terrorist attacks, including: by strengthening military helicopter preparedness, which may, upon request, provide assistance to the police. We also want the aid instructions to be anchored in law, as the government is working on. I would also like to mention the importance of new emergency networks – also in the light of good terrorist preparedness. A Joint Committee concludes on the follow-up and action of the main findings of the 22 July Commission on management, attitudes and culture. We are very concerned that new, useful and efficient technologies should be put into practice, not least to have the most effective police force possible. The Committee appears to have contributed several interesting input and ideas to the Technology Council during its hearing. The Council has an ongoing project, security and transparency after 22 July, which provides knowledge that should be useful in the work of the Police Directorate on a comprehensive ICT strategy. That's about. 700,000 hearing impaired in Norway. The Committee stresses the importance that hearing impaired and deaf people can communicate in direct contact with emergency situations, using SMS, as soon as this is possible, as the Council of State has clearly indicated that it will take care of them – by the end of 2015. I would also like to mention that the majority of the Committee supports the government's approach to new arms law. The Progress Party stands alone with its proposal that the government refrain from restricting today’s weapons legislation. We are all keenly concerned that it must be a top priority to set up a response centre in Oslo for the national police response capacities. This requires that the regulatory plan be clear during 2014 so that the Centre can be established by 1 January 2018. At the same time, it is also important that basic police preparedness throughout the country be increased, as the government proposes, through the deployment of police emergency services in the districts, increased training for the police to deal with ‘shooting is underway’ episodes, and the establishment of minimum staff for all police operating centres. Furthermore, as an important contribution to identifying, averting and ensuring good prevention and preparedness throughout Norway, it is important that police districts have a staff of PSTs that are satisfactory in order to do a good job. A single committee considers this to be a point of reference. This, too, needs to be focused when the overall use of resources by the police is put under scrutiny. In the context of both major messages, the Committee also draws attention to the major and demanding information security challenges we face. When most of our society is so dependent on ICT, we naturally also become more vulnerable – to attacks and crime. That vulnerability can and must be reduced. The Committee stresses the importance of education and research in this field. All the good forces must go together, and do so, through the establishment of a national centre at the Gjøvik High School, also highlighted by the special committee here on the Stortinget. The Centre shall conduct research and development of competences in the field of information security, with national and international partners. The Committee considers this to be an important establishment. Norway has been hit by a number of serious incidents in recent years and social security work is a very broad field. It also bears the impression - a setting in which we have otherwise captured many good input from the committee hearing. We have made good advice and views – through the suspension – into national policies. There is a broad consensus on most of what is needed to prevent and manage natural events, major accidents and intentional incidents – such as terrorism. The Social Security Communication introduces the interoperability principle as the new and fourth principle in social security and preparedness work, as well as equality, responsibility and proximity. The Committee supports this. At the same time, the Committee is committed to ensuring that good cooperation also requires clear management in order to optimise the functioning of the various actors individually and collectively. The central crisis management has been strengthened and the role of the Ministry of Justice and Security in coordination and leadership has been strengthened and clarified. The Social Security Communication describes many measures that have been taken, in particular in the light of the experience of 22 July 2011 – and time does not allow me to go into this detail here and now. I would therefore like to briefly describe some of the points that the Committee has raised and pointed out, which we believe contribute to new steps in the right direction. Overall, there is considerable resources in Norwegian society in the form of personnel and equipment, and we are getting better when these resources are seen in the context. Cooperation between emergency situations, police, health, fire and rescue shall be further developed – and it is said to be in good cooperation with the voluntary rescue and rescue organisations. The rescue service in Norway is unique and volunteers constitute a carrier. They represent an impressive spirit of effort, capacity and competence that the Committee believes is very important to maintain and contribute to further development — yes, to renew energy. To this end, the Committee shall enhance the position and capacity of these voluntary organisations. There is a clear signal to the municipalities and emergency authorities about the importance of good cooperation, through agreements, common procedures and cooperation with this important part of Freedom Norway. Then the results are also the best when it comes to it. They also have a role to play in making local analyses and plans – they have useful knowledge and experience. These organisations have 20,000 active members. I think it's great that KS is now signaling increased attention to social security and preparedness. Let it also include even better partnerships with volunteers in this field. That's my call. I hope that the involvement of the KS will help several municipalities to start freeing employees with pay to participate in rescue operations and emergency efforts. We understand that practical problems can sometimes arise in the workplace, and that it is not always easy – yet it is of great value to have employees with such skills and willingness to intervene. That call is also addressed to private employers, of course. These relevant organisations, Red Cross, Norwegian Public Aid, the voluntary part of the Rescue Company and Norwegian Rescue Dogs – the four who will have to cover all their expenses related to their arrival on emergency networks – are totally dependent on stable and predictable framework conditions in order to do their job and be prepared 24 hours a day. We must then ensure that they have such framework conditions, and that is clearly stated by the Committee. Otherwise, we support the establishment of major intercommunal fire and rescue companies to strengthen the ability to deal with increasingly demanding tasks, to strengthen prevention, the supervisory function, competence and impact. Today, these companies cover about 1.5 million of the country's inhabitants. I guess we're actually saying -- a little bit interpreted from here -- that we want more of these companies. We are also concerned about increasing the focus on fire and rescue skills, and we are particularly pointing to this in the more than 8 000 part-time teams. Fire and rescue services must be good in all parts of the country — urban and rural. The Committee points out that the intentions of the part-time reform must be followed up. The Committee also unanimously supports the continuation of civil protection, while stressing that it must be given the necessary resources. The water level is still high in many places. The social security attitude refers to the flood of the year. Our main message is that the emergency need to be lifted – yes, get what I will call increased authority in all relevant planning. Current plans need to be reviewed where necessary, and there should be a low threshold to consider critically the plan. Furthermore, we say – and it is important to note – that if major flood damage has first occurred, the Committee will emphasise that it is a national objective to include the prevention and mitigation of floods in the recovery of the damage. For many municipalities, risk and vulnerability analyses and contingency plans do not exist. It's actually a crime. At the same time, so it is also said that many municipalities are good at this. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e565	What can we do to make Norway an even safer country? This issue is behind us when we are now discussing today’s and the future’s threat picture – and its consequences. In the right, it is highly critical that the government had not sufficiently implemented measures that enabled society and the state to protect its citizens. For this reason, the Right had clear expectations that, in particular, the notification of terrorist preparedness would be a crystal clear strategy and a good tool for better security. The right is therefore surprised and disappointed that the staging message is so little concrete. There is a review of many issues that have not been addressed, draft legislation that has not yet been drafted, and issues that remain to be assessed. However, it has been important for the Right to facilitate a policy that will bring together the Biggest on a key direction that will increase social security and better preparedness for terrorism. For the Right, the main profile has been particularly focused on making visible and clarifying responsibilities – also at the very top. We have focused on police and defence cooperation. And in particular, we have raised the need for increased security in the cyber domain – in the opinion of the Right, probably the area where the threat scene evolves most in a threatening direction. I would like to thank the rapporteur, who has arranged for an inclusive process in the Committee's examination of these matters. In the opinion of the Right, it is high time to review the traditional civil-military sector distinction. The historical justification for this distinction is important to remember, but today’s threat picture needs to be met with a greater degree of coordinated efforts by the country’s security agencies. In today’s threat assessments, there is no longer a crystal-clear distinction between national and international threats. This is why the right has several times proposed the creation of a separate law that can provide a predictable and effective way for a good interaction between police and defence – within the framework of the Constitution. These proposals have been voted down, but we note with satisfaction that the government has announced that it will still produce such a law. A large and growing part of the social structure is based on digital data and computer systems. It makes society more efficient, and it facilitates business development and good services. But at the same time it makes us more vulnerable. It is therefore important to strengthen information security. The right is surprised that this isn't dedicated to greater space in the matters we're discussing here today. Cyberdomain is an area where the traditional civil-military distinction between national and international is almost meaningless. The cyber domain is boundless. Therefore, the right considers that responsibility for information security should be brought together in one department. We had in this field expected offensive measures and a forward-looking discussion, but we did not. On the right, support the establishment of a Emergency Response Centre where part of the police special areas of expertise are co-located. But we are concerned about the government's approach. It is an impression that the site is first determined and then adapted to the operation of the emergency centre according to the appropriateness of the site. So when we can also read about uncertainty about progress and costs, we are in favour of a proposal to get on the table here in Stortinget a review. In addition, the right notes that several places in the country are very committed to pooling preparedness resources, establishing areas of practice and strengthening environments of competence. I mention Rygge, Bodø, Stavanger, Buskerud and Akershus. This is positive, and the Right expects all recordings to be carefully evaluated by the Ministry of Justice. Finally, I would like to stress that with this treatment, we have facilitated a safer society. At the same time, we have to remember that we never get a risk-free society, but we can always work for a risk reduction. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e692	In the Terrorist Preparedness Communication, the Government sets out both new measures and the work already under way to improve preparedness, and we put them into an overall strategy against terrorism. I am pleased that there is broad political consensus, both on how the strategy is put up and on the measures. I have always experienced cooperation with the Stortinget to improve preparedness as very constructive, both with the Special Committee, the Control Committee and the Constitutional Committee and now with the Justice Committee. In the Communication on Social Security, the government introduced the principle of interoperability as a fourth guiding principle for preparedness in Norway. It is also the interoperability principle based on the strategy: we need to have the most out of society's overall response capacities, so that the whole is greater than the sum of the parts. This is a good social economy. What, then, does the cooperative principle mean in practice? The local police will usually come first to an incident and form our basic preparedness. That's why we're increasing the number of police officers participating in local emergency services, from today's 800 to 1,200 in the current year and next year. And we're increasing the amount of training for, let's call it, regular police — the largest group in the police force with weapons approval — by eight hours a year, earmarked to deal with ‘shooting in progress’ situations. However, when dealing with a crisis or a terrorist attack, we need structures in place to ensure that personnel with sufficient resources can strengthen local efforts and reach most of the population in Norway within a reasonable time. The government has prepared guidelines for the rescue helicopters standing on six bases around the country to be able to transport police in time-critical operations. And we've arranged for the police to attract exchange of defence resources when a crisis strikes. Both at Rygge and Bardufoss, military helicopters are ready to transport police forces. At Rygge, military forces can also assist the police in resolving counter-terrorism tasks — so-called enforcement assistance. The government wants to draw the cooperation between police and defence anymore. Therefore, the Marine Hunters' Command from 1 August will also be prepared to assist the police in counter-terrorist missions. From the outset, the Special Command of the Defense is ready to assist the police. So, in order for cooperation to work well when a crisis occurs, good practice is needed. That is why the police and the defense have trained on interaction during three major exercises after July 22nd. Experience with operational cooperation during these exercises is positive. During the exercise of Gemini last month, police director Odd Reidar Hoplegård said to the NRK that cooperation between police and defence is better than ever. Norwegian counter-terrorism preparedness has not been better than it is now. The cooperative principle will only work well in practice if someone has looked at and sees the whole of the preparedness process. For this reason, the Government has given the Ministry of Justice and Defence a clearer responsibility to coordinate work on social security and preparedness in the departments and underlying agencies. I would remind you that the 22nd July Commission recommended that it be clear where responsibility for preparedness lies. Since there are ‘close links between police tasks and the general security and preparedness of society’, responsibility should lie with the Ministry of Justice and Defence. Soon two years have passed since July 22nd. The message summarises all actions undertaken and all new resources involved in the preparedness process. In sum, it's very much. If someone with police preparedness or other agencies goes into the media and says that nothing has improved, do we, as politicians, have to ask some critical questions: Do you, for example, take work to address the cultural challenges raised by the July 22 Commission in a sufficiently serious way? Time is getting closer to turning the issue around: we have to expect a better preparedness. We have to expect that people with responsibility for emergency care are the task of adulthood. We have to expect that we will get value for the money that is granted. In a short time, I'll receive a police analysis. It will give us answers to a number of questions: Are resources used correctly? Is the police organized in a manner consistent with the crime scene? Do we have professional environments that are strong enough? The police analysis will provide politicians with a solid professional basis for taking the long-term decisions on choice for the police. It will also provide a comprehensive overview of the use of resources by the police in relation to ICT. As the Commission also points out on 22 July, ‘the potential of information and communication technologies has not been sufficiently exploited’ in the police. [[...]] The government has advocated the renewal of police ICT systems in criminal matters. The renewal is carried out in the so-called Value Added Programme. It is divided into several quality assurance projects separately. It gives politicians good control of the process. At the same time, the Police Directorate is given the opportunity to build the necessary skills throughout the entire department, so that all employees are prepared to take up the new solutions when they get in place. However, the Value Added Tax Programme must not become a sleeper cushion. This will not solve all the ICT challenges in the police. For example, we have a police force where every police district and special body has its own pay and staff system. This is the picture of a major challenge: the police have lacked an overall plan; the approach to ICT has been fragmented and divided. One has not been able to raise the best practice and make it the standard for the whole police. The police director has done a good job of setting up a separate department for ICT in the Directorate. But we need more radical changes on the ICT side of the police. To avert terrorist acts before they get a chance to take place, we need a police security service that is well equipped to carry out its community mission. The PST increased its financial envelope from 434 million. SEK in 2011 to nearly 525 million. In 2012, much of the increase went to counter-terrorism. The budget has been increased by a further 41.6 million. kr in year. The PST has had a huge impact on its input into budgetary processes. The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party claim that ‘only 91 million are used. crowns for combating terrorism’ in the PST. Of course, this is not right. Expenditure from several other items in the budget — e.g. to investigate, investigate and analyse, including counter-terrorism. In reality, PST spends much more on counter-terrorism than the 91 million. kr to which reference is made. Also in the case of PST, we have to make sure that the money is used correctly. Therefore, the government reduced the Traavik Committee which conducted an external assessment of the PST last year. We are now working to follow up the recommendations of the sample, in particular with regard to how the PST can make better use of its resources, make good priorities and plan to acquire the right competences for the future. With the message of preparedness for terrorism, we have taken a big step. But we can never lean back and say that we're in the goal. We must always reach out for the best possible preparedness in our country.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e798	Today's debate and settings have a dark backdrop. On 22 July 2011, Norway was hit by the worst acts of terrorism since the war. 77 people lost their lives, many more were injured, and the material damage was enormous. Even though it is nearly two years since the terrible thing happened, it is missed after the dead still large. Many of those affected are still suffering physical and mental damage. We feel with the survivors, the survivors and the relatives. The July 22 Commission described in detail the failure: the failure to implement already adopted security measures, the lack of implementation of terrorist and emergency plans, poor communication and management, slowing police exit to Utøya. The Commission wrote that the problems were not primarily the lack of resources, legislation or organisation, but cultures, attitudes to preparedness and the ability to interact. At the same time, it is necessary to recall that much of the rescue operation on 22 July has received legal recognition. The fire and rescue services, the ambulance services, hospitals and health professionals made a formidable, life-saving effort in the government quarter and Utøya. Many volunteers contributed to lifesaving and rescue in the government quarter and Utøya. After such a dramatic and traumatic terrorist action, it is very important that criticism, experience, evaluations and proposals for action are carefully reviewed and evaluated. Changes must be made. What has failed needs to be improved. What worked shall be further strengthened. We know that this will take time – changing the culture, attitudes and structures of large organisations is not done overnight. In the last year, the government has been making an offensive effort and has done a great deal to strengthen the preparedness for terrorism and social security in Norway. The emergency centre of Alnabru has been decided upon. Strengthening the police helicopter service, PST, police response personnel and Marines on national alerts are other examples. By March, the government presented a proposal to strengthen social security in general and police preparedness in particular. The selection to analyse the challenges of the Norwegian police will present its position in a few weeks’ time, which has already been a topic in the debate here. Together with today’s positions, the police analysis will help form the basis for the organisation of the police structure, preparedness and staffing of the future. The goal is the most effective close-up police force possible. People have to feel that the police are present in their community. Structures, organisation, staffing and equipment must facilitate this. In the event of serious incidents, local police are usually the first to emerge, and therefore the organisation of the police must be robust, so that for example: Operation centres are manned in a way that enables them to deal with major crises. Good emergency systems require clear command line and good communication. There must be clear responsibilities between the SMK, the departments and the Directorates. Defence assistance to the police in crisis situations needs to be further developed. It is positive that we now have a national police operation central and a mass recall system for police personnel. I have noticed that the voluntary rescue organisations are being raised in the adjustment. Organisations such as Red Cross, Norwegian People's Aid, the Rescue Company and Norwegian Rescue Dogs are making a very important social effort. In total, there are about 20,000 active volunteers, and we see daily examples of what these mean, for example. under the flood now recently. We value them, next to the municipal emergency equipment and the emergency kits. The voluntary rescue organisations shall now be included in the emergency network. It is unfortunately impossible to create a risk-free society. We do not want a police state where everything and everyone is monitored. We want an open, democratic society with a short distance between the rulers and the rulers. Our aim is to minimise the risk of terrorism and violence by preventing, monitoring, averting and tackling it.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e819	The security of the population is one of the country's most remote tasks. We must build on the four principles of preparedness: the principle of responsibility, the principle of subsidiarity, the principle of equality and the principle of interoperability. However, when we are going to carry out the most important tasks, namely the safety of citizens, there is a need for knowledge to be shared between different departments and emergency services. Therefore, it is also important that there is a clear and clear responsibility to follow up developments in the field at the Prime Minister's office. In order to clean up the police role, preparedness will be significantly improved. Where police stations and janitorial offices are located close to each other, it may in the long term make sense to merge them into larger and more robust units. And as other speakers have said before, we look forward to the recommendations that come next week. But it is also necessary that we have a good, specialty police, and since it may take time for the police to expect assistance from the specialty sections, we need to have operative police officers who have enough training to handle serious incidents all over the country. For the defence, it is necessary to clarify that it is done quickly, that we lower the thresholds and remove the bottlenecks, so that the police can provide assistance as quickly as possible. Today, treatment is one step further in making Norway a safer place and in improving preparedness, but the long-term preparedness focus is not just a matter of decision-making here; it is a matter of continuous development of the management culture and of keeping alert at the forefront of the panic attack when decisions are taken today.

Therefore, requests for assistance must also be made, and action must be taken when the situation so warrants. At the same time, the Committee would point out that it is a challenge that this helicopter preparedness may be reduced for periods due to other priority missions or activation of NATO response forces. In such a situation, the Committee considers that it is necessary to assess whether remedial measures need to be implemented in order to maintain the readiness of this allocation. When we think about readiness, we have to think about preparedness across the country.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1041	We are now considering a proposal by representatives from the Right. The proposal is to ask the government: «sooner, propose to replace the Housing Entry Act guarantee scheme with an insurance scheme providing for stronger consumer protection, while reducing the negative impact of equity and loan difficulties on the construction industry.» So this is the wording of the proposal. The majority of the Committee, the Workers' Party, the SV and the Centre, do not support the proposal. I believe that the majority itself will argue for its conclusion. As I see it, it is important that the legislation we have does not prevent operators in the construction industry from building dwellings or that the legislation makes housing unnecessarily expensive. In the case of the purchase and sale of a new dwelling, the contractor is required to provide a guarantee of performance of the contract. The provision on guarantee was amended in 2010 by the law on changes in the law on residence entry and disposal. The change took place on the basis of a proposal from the government of Stoltenberg II to strengthen the requirements for the guarantees. In short, the warranty period was extended from two to five years after the acquisition of the dwelling, and the amount of the guarantees increased from 5 to 10 pst. of the consideration. The impact of the change is emerging, and this is the case in the first few smaller enterprises. Several fears now that smaller construction companies will not be able to provide guarantees in the future. This is something shared by the minority of the committee. That is why the minority of the Committee, the Party of Advances and the Right, wants to join the draft representative. These effects may mean that firms need to be closed down and that this will be particularly true in the districts, but it may also have indirect consequences in terms of more expensive and slower housing. The costs of the guarantee scheme are shifted to the consumer in the form of more expensive dwellings. The guarantee scheme does not provide adequate security to the dwelling builder, as the undersigned sees it. This applies primarily to consumers with the greatest need for protection, namely those who have major housing problems. Therefore, it is urgent to put in place an insurance system capable of providing for faster and cheaper housing, which can prevent smaller construction firms from being closed down or unable to build to the extent that they have the potential to do so and which provides consumers with better rights in the event of housing errors. I'll take up the minority proposal in the vote.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1063	The representative Ulf Leirstein has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1066	The proposers shall be praised for promoting a case with a view to strengthening consumer rights in the construction of housing. However, when the Workers' Party and the Red-Green parties do not join the proposal, it has a bearing on our uncertainty as to whether this is the right way to go. We are also concerned about these consumer issues, but it indicates that a tightening of the warranty rules has already been implemented, and there is also significant regulation of resale. This amendment entered into force on 1 July 2011 and before an evaluation or assessment of the impact of this enhancement has been carried out, there are several elements that need to be assessed before an insurance scheme can be implemented. I also show that Stortinget has said that a new assessment of the guarantee rules must be carried out within five years of the promise entered into force. The changes made so far, including: strengthening the warranty requirements from two to five years and regulation of resales, may result in higher housing prices. The bankruptcy and closure of small and medium-sized enterprises can therefore be a short-term consequence. In the next round, this could lead to an even larger company that is already large today. Such developments may also lead to monopolistic conditions in the housing market. This won't be on fire. We believe that consumers are well cared for by current legislation, but the consequence may be that the market price may be higher. In some parts of the country housing prices are already high enough. If a new insurance scheme were to be introduced, this would also have a cost side that would be passed on to consumers. Against this background, I would suggest that the matter be annexed to the Protocol and that one should first carry out the assessment to be made after the Storting decided to strengthen the guarantee rules and regulated for the resale of dwellings.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1081	Four out of five residents own their own dwelling in Norway, which is the highest proportion of ownership in the Nordic region and among the highest in the world. Purchases of owner-occupied dwellings are the largest single investment ordinary people make, and housing purchases constitute a long-term and costly investment for the consumer. This means that good consumer rights are important in the construction of new dwellings. At the same time, we need to make sure that we do not have a code that is designed to compromise housing. According to the SSB, the Central Statistical Office, in 2012, the construction of 30 189 dwellings was registered — a significant number. However, it is currently being built approx. 10 000 dwellings are too small in Norway per year. In our view, the current guarantee system for construction defects is neither good enough for consumers nor most appropriate from a development and business point of view. The provisions of the Housing Entry Act on guarantee of performance of the contract were amended in 2010. In short, the warranty period was extended from two to five years after the takeover of the dwelling, and the amount of the guarantees increased from 5 to 10 pst. of the consideration. Both the construction industry and the Right warned at the time that the change could have an adverse effect on business operators in the construction industry, because it could create equity and loan difficulties. As a result of the new arrangement, the equity requirement of the enterprises has in reality quadrupled because the guarantee requirements are imposed on each other by new housing projects during the five-year period. The impact of this is now in the process of becoming apparent – first for smaller enterprises. There is reason to fear that many smaller construction companies will not be able to provide guarantees in the future. This may mean that companies have to be closed down, and this can be particularly true in the districts. Indirectly, we can also see consequences in terms of more expensive and slower housing. Thus, the costs of the guarantee scheme are shifted to the consumer in the form of more expensive dwellings. If the guarantee scheme had provided sufficient security to the consumer, we could understand that the government believes it is so important to maintain the change in the guarantee provision. However, as we see it, the guarantee system does not provide sufficient protection – even after it has been reinforced. Those who fail in the first place are those consumers who need the most protection, namely those who feel that the housing defects are large. Even though it doesn't happen often, it happens that we see errors in the million class. Therefore, as the proposers see it, the guarantee scheme should be replaced by an insurance scheme. The fact that consumers are assured through insurance schemes will not become any strange thing, such schemes are known from among others. Denmark. An insurance scheme may provide for faster and cheaper housing, while preventing small construction firms from cutting or being unable to build to the extent that they have the potential to do so and giving consumers better rights in the event of housing errors. The Progress Party and the Right propose that a legislative proposal to replace the Housing Entry Act guarantee scheme with an insurance scheme providing for greater consumer protection should be put forward as soon as possible, while at the same time reducing the negative impact of equity and loan difficulties on the construction industry.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1098	Purchases of new dwellings are a major and important investment for most consumers, and therefore good consumer rights are important in this area. However, as this case shows, the extent to which insurance schemes should be established requires strict balances between the need to protect consumers in the event of non-compliance by the contractor and the need to avoid an excessive increase in construction costs. Infringement of the guarantee rules by Law No 10 December 2010 74 on changes in the law of residence entry and disposal constituted a compromise between these considerations. The fact that the legislative changes would result in a certain increase in the cost to contractors of the provision of guarantees was highlighted in the proposal as an unavoidable consequence of strengthening consumer protection in the event of increased guarantee requirements. Therefore, when the Committee's minority is calling for the replacement of the right of residence guarantee scheme by an insurance scheme, I consider it appropriate to stress that an insurance scheme will also have a cost side that raises balancing issues of this type. The question of an insurance scheme for construction defects was assessed in NOU 2005: 12 More efficient building legislation II. The selection concluded here that it would not recommend an insurance scheme as an alternative to the Housing Entry Act. The committee considered that the Housing Entry Act provided for the need to regulate guarantee and insurance schemes for construction defects, but that the statutory guarantee provisions should be reviewed in view of the extension of the amount and duration of the guarantee. This has now been followed up by the strengthening of the guarantee rules, which entered into force on 1 July 2011. On this basis, I agree with the majority of the Committee that it now seems not appropriate to consider the issue of any insurance scheme and that an assessment of the guarantee rules should be awaited in line with the conditions set out in the Innst. 74 L for 2010–2011. It was considered that, on the basis of experience gained from various actors in the field, a new assessment should be available within five years of the entry into force of the enhanced guarantee requirements.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1111	I would like to stress that what we do not discuss in this case is the consumer's ability to get the right solution. If the guarantee scheme becomes effective, it is provided by the bank, and then the money to cover any construction errors comes from the bank. If we have an insurance scheme, the money to cover construction defects, which is for consumers, comes from an insurance company. So consumers are just as well off whether the money comes from insurance or from a bank. What made me take the floor was that the representative called up Hansen actually admitted that today's system, where one is rigidly bound to the guarantee scheme, had the effect that it could trigger bankruptcy in smaller construction companies. It was supposed to be completely unnecessary. An insurance scheme could otherwise relieve the risk between contractors without the need to reserve parts of its equity. The way we set ourselves up now means that entrepreneurs with sound equity, by their size, have a competitive advantage that does not promote competition, nor does the efficient use of resources, and thus does not help to keep prices down through competition – to the detriment of the consumer, of course. I also think that the Minister of Justice's answer was very formalistic. One feels bounded by a five-year limit to assess this. Now it was the maximum time to assess, and there is nothing in the way that the Storting can go through its own legislation if the industry reports back that this has unintended effects – effects that the industry correctly warned against in advance but that are now apparent in the ground when the scheme is implemented. Then it must be in the policy that errors can be fixed faster than keeping to theoretical limits of five years. It is also one of my dear postulates that the difference between public operations and private operations is not that the public always makes mistakes and the private never makes mistakes, but that the private is at least able to correct mistakes much faster. I also think the Stortinget as legislator should be. Therefore, there would have been an advantage for consumers, for industry and for lower housing prices if the minority's proposals had been adopted here today.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1170	I am afraid the previous speakers have misunderstood the whole foundation of the proposals of the Progress Party, and I will come back to that. One of the main prerequisites of the rule of law is an independent court. The starting point for the principle of power distribution is that the three state powers, independently of each other, exercise the powers conferred on them by the Constitution. However, after the occupation of parliamentarianism in 1884, a strong transfer of power really took place from executive to elected power. These developments have intensified in a number of ways. The size is the highest popularly elected level, and a development in which the elected state power has a somewhat different position than the two non-elected state powers appears today to be quite reasonable. The proposal to involve Stortinget in the appointment of Supreme Court judges is intended to better protect citizens' rights. It is the Storting which is the representatives of the citizens, while the executive has a completely different and more limited function. A government is dependent on not meeting mistrust in the Storting, and is therefore dependent on the Storting in order to do its constitutional duty under the Constitution. However, the same is not the case in the courts, despite the fact that the Supreme Court, often to a greater extent than the government, affects the actual state of law by its judicial and judicial powers. In today's system, a lawyer with ambitions about the seat of the Supreme Court depends on standing in a favorite light towards the executive, who has the right of appointment only after the suspension of the Constitutional Council – that is, opposite to what the representative Ljunggren here tried to give the impression of. The state power of the citizens, the elected population, is completely excluded from this process. Nor, despite the insinuations about the opposite, does the Storting have any real control function over the government's appointment of judges of the Supreme Court, both in the Ljunggren report and from the Council of State in dealing with this matter. It is unmistakable that through its subsequent control, the Storting could influence the appointment of judges to the Supreme Court. At best, the Storting could throw a government council for making formal mistakes in an appointment or if the Storing does not have confidence in the functioning of the state council. It will not affect the composition of the third state power. It is obvious that it is right when the ministry believes that democratic considerations can speak in order to involve the Stortinge in the appointment process. We live in a democracy. I am therefore surprised at the conclusion when, in the next turn, the Department believes that the danger of party political appointments means ignoring democracy. It must be due to a misunderstanding when the majority believes that today's appointment process is less political than a process in which the country's citizens are heard through their representatives on the Stortinge in front of the appointment. Today, in principle, the government is free to appoint judges, preferably on the basis of the political stance of the government at all times. There are ways to do it, despite the fact that the Configuration Council is independent. It is today's system that gives rise to suspicion of any party political appointment. By involving the Stortinget, as a prior approval of several candidates, the entire party political aspect of the nominations is removed, and both the other two state powers, both the elected and the executive, are involved in the process rather than independent judges of the third state power. It is – regardless of what we may think – that recent studies show that the background of judges has a significant impact on their judicial activities. In the future, the courts will be exposed to an even more critical searchlight than they are already exposed to today. This could lead some to claim that governments exploit the appointment of judges to the highest court in that country in order to ensure the effectiveness of political fundamentals by future Supreme Court judges when they themselves have to leave government offices. There appears to be little insight when the department's response is based on a previous discussion about adding judges' appointments to the Stortinget. This proposal is definitely not about that, so I'm a little uncertain whether the Council of State has bothered to read the proposal before sending answers to the committee. Unfortunately, it is – we now experience it as well – that many important constitutional debates in this room are reduced to questions about what is policy and what is not policy. Democracy is about politics, it's all about politics. It becomes no more or less political that the government has more power over the so-called third and independent state power than the elected assembly. I hope in the future we can take such discussions with a slightly more open mind than what I think has happened during the examination of this proposal, not least after what was confirmed in the speeches by the representative Ljunggren, for the starting point here is to create an unpolitical process until the appointment of an even more independent and competent Supreme Court. I would therefore like to bring up the proposal of the Advance Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1302	The proposals we are considering today are essentially a follow-up to the 22nd July committee at Stortinget and the position where we asked that the legislative proposals that PST had sent to the ministry should be sent to the hearing. These various legislative proposals were widely commented on in the hearing. There were many important recordings, and the government continued to work with them until the legislative proposals were promoted. We also have here today – in the same way as we did in the vote of 22 July – a unanimous vote by the Justice Committee. It is gratifying and positive that we have a common approach to these important issues. Although there is a unanimous vote, I see reason to comment on some of the content of the proposals and some of the processes underlying the final result. The first point I want to comment on concerns preparations for acts of terror done by what are called solo terrorists – others have called them lonely wolves, self-radicalized people, etc. – which does not have links to large terrorist networks, or which have deliberately hidden these bands, so that only the individuals can be seen when trying to discover what they are preparing – i.e. preparations that are not made in alliances. We have had provisions from 2002 to prepare in leagues, but not solo terrorists or individuals. What we're doing now is we're going to introduce a law that also affects them. It requires some important factors that are further defined in the legislative proposal, namely that it must be a pattern of material preparations and preparatory elements before one can talk about the preparation of a solo terrorist act. The second element is that it also has to be a deliberate, that this pattern and other things make it possible to prove that it is a deliberate act of terror. This means that these – a kind of – parodys drawn from the bill in public debates earlier, that it was enough to have rubber gloves, etc. in order to be affected by this provision, nothing is in line with the proposal itself, which presupposes these two important elements which I have now mentioned. It was useful for the Judicial Committee during the hearing that we had worked on this important bill, to note that the national lawyer commented that there had been important improvements in the bill as it is now being promoted, and that he was surprised that his comments or input to the bill had been placed as negative. He also commented that he had not done his job sufficiently in terms of sharing experience with solo terrorism in the past. We think we have a reassuring proposal from the committee on this point, and that is a unanimous vote here. I would also like to comment on a few other points in the legislative proposals, where an important effort has been made to get it accurate and correct. This concerns the receipt of terrorist training in training camps. It also needs to be criminalised, but it requires that you know that it is terrorist training – that is, it is not like any other training to use firearms, etc., but that you understand that the purpose of training is to train terrorists. What we didn't include in the elements that were out at the hearing was that you could be judged for receiving terrorist training if you were in areas where there was such a terrorist training. So in general, it's not formulated here, it requires that you receive terrorist training yourself, and that you know that the purpose of that training is to train terrorists. I see that there is already a red light – we have also made the right legislative proposals to extend criminal liability for the possession of weapons and explosives, and insert a provision contained in the Law of 2005 into the 1902 Act, which also expands the elements with which we can be judged to have a hold. The same applies to the criminalisation of qualified participation in terrorist organisations, which are also further defined in the bill. We are extending the penal framework for serious acts of terrorism to 30 years. There is already a provision in the 2005 Act, and we are implementing an extension of the scope of Section 60 on organised crime.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1331	The rapporteur has explained the setting in a good way, although he may have been getting a little too short. What is important here is to see that this is largely about prevention and the belief in preventing terrorism. That is why this is important, especially in these times when you consider who is monitoring who. It's a big debate around it. The Committee therefore points out in particular that: «COMMUNICATION Article 7 and the principle of legality of the Basic Law § 96 require that criminal provisions must describe the criminal relationship with a precision which reasonably permits the prediction of the conduct that is punishable.» It's a balance. Some people have suggested that we make a law to punish what people think about. But it's up to the courts and the police to show the precision that the committee also points to. I record in today's VG that the Minister of Justice fully agrees with the proposal. The Minister of Justice also expressed the satisfaction that the undersigned now joins in her wish to extend the limitation periods. To that end, I must be allowed to say that the Progress Party promoted this proposal in 2004, and I think we also repeated it by new criminal law in 2005. If someone joins someone here, let's not make it a big discussion. It is the matter that is important, and it is important in the sense that there are many very serious criminal cases that are at risk of disappearing, even if today you have methods and opportunities, if you give resources to the police, to take up, investigate and perhaps find people who have committed serious crime. Proposal No. 1 concerns the extension of the limitation periods. The background to the rules on limitation is twofold. Firstly, evidence will be difficult to obtain as time goes on, and it is difficult to see that this will be a good independent argument in more serious criminal cases, the Progress Party believes today – in 2013. The presumption of innocence already means that there is a threshold of proof when the prosecution authorities can take action. If the evidence isn't good enough, it won't be charged anyway. It speaks to eliminate and increase the limitation periods. On the other hand, limitation is a definitive and strong instrument for cutting off criminal proceedings. If the case is outdated, it is finally closed without the possibility of a discretionary weighing. On the other hand, such remedies in the specific case would be considered by the Court of Justice if an earlier case were considered. It is fully possible to emphasise the age of the evidence in their assessment. On the other hand, a limitation is finally without the possibility of reasonableness assessments in the specific case. Secondly, society and the offender should see themselves done with a case as time passes. While these are important arguments in the area of money demand law, it is clear to the proposers that their views should be significantly weaker and perhaps eliminated in the event of serious crime. It's hard to see the value of the possibility that a killer should be able to put the killings behind him without being punished. Society should also have a strong interest in law enforcement, especially in the more serious cases. If the Storting joins this proposal today, I think we will give a lot of people, relatives and victims, a handshake with regard to their despair that the matter can be outdated. I am promoting this proposal by the Progress Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1383	Let me first join in the very clear, strong and clear emphasis of the committee chairman on the level of precision in the application of the laws that we are to adopt today. That is an important clarification said from the Parliamentary Chair. This is an important issue concerning the security and prevention of terrorism of our society. Here we're going to have a law that captures the developments that are taking place not only in our country, but also in the global community. Today the world is much smaller. The conflicts around the world are multidimensional and of a global nature. Take, for example: Afghanistan. It's a remote country in a different part of the world than ours. A few decades ago, we had a different focus on this country than what we have today. Today we have our soldiers and our civilians in Afghanistan. Thus, the conflicts that exist in Afghanistan and neighbouring countries have direct implications for us and our security. Syria is another country that lies far away from us, but the conflict that has now developed such a character that it can have consequences for our own security here at home. Unfortunately, the conflicts in both these countries and in other countries that are implicated in these conflicts have attracted our young people. They have been inspired by violence, weapons training and other undesirable training that, in worst cases, can be used to disrupt our social order, our security and our values. We have to take this seriously. At the same time, unfortunately, we have also experienced a terrorist act on our earth committed by a «homegrown» man who in quiet prepared terror, and unfortunately was not revealed until he succeeded in carrying out his evil plan. Some believe that he could have gone free even though he was discovered before carrying out the terrorist action, which turned out to be fatal. Of course, this should not be said. This requires strengthening the police and the PST with increased resources and better management, but at the same time the legislation has in place to provide the police with the necessary tools to prevent, detect, investigate and promote prosecution where necessary. It is important that, when we equip the police – and especially the PST – with extended framework conditions, we be careful not to conflict with the freedoms they are just about to defend. The freedom to think freely is crucial here. We are proposing today to criminalise the planning of terrorist acts, to receive training in terrorist acts, to recruit others to terrorist organisations and to support terrorist organisations by collecting money for them. In addition, we are criminalising ourselves to acquire weapon parts that can be used collectively in a terrorist act. Today, we are laying new frameworks and creating new barriers to the fight against crime in our democracy. The aim is to protect our fundamental values: freedom of expression and the right to organise freely. Therefore, it is important in this context for SV to say that intentions and thoughts about carrying out a terrorist act must be supported by the external evidence, which must prove beyond any doubt that someone intended to carry out a terrorist act. Otherwise, you can quickly get into a situation where thoughts are banned and criminalized. It would be a ban that conflicts with the very basis of our democracy and the basis of the laws we will adopt today. There's reason to remind you of wise words from Benjamin Franklin. He said in his day: ‘Those who wish to give up essential freedom to purchase a small temporary security do not deserve either freedom or security.’

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1439	We're facing a terrorist threat that's hard to deal with. The first concerns the transparency of information such as: has led to a case being raised – the entrance to a case, as it is called – in the police's work. There may be different sources, different informants, from which the police have received information, which are not to be used as evidence in any way in the case, but which have been the start and given the incentive to the police, for example. has initiated an investigation. For a long time, there has been a problem that the police have not been able to provide these sources with predictability — avoiding them being exposed to threats and represals — in view of how this information will be processed during any judicial process. Now we are clarifying that the law, as proposed here today by a unanimous judicial committee, is not to be made transparent in this type of information which is not to be provided as evidence, if this could lead to the disclosure of the identity of a person who has provided information to the police. In other words, there is no need to disclose anything that could lead to it in legal proceedings. It is also the case that this provision is to be followed by the individual judge who is in a position to do so — in the past there was a different wording. It is also the case that the penal framework requirement for refusing access to this type of information is lifted, so that it can also be used in cases other than where there are high penalties, because these threats and the harsh violence against those who provide information also apply in cases where there are not so high penalties. We have come up with a proposal here that I believe meets the task of ensuring the police's access to information in order to combat crime better, while at the same time we have included it within the provisions, including those relating to it. of course, everything to be relied on as evidence shall be presented in the same way as today. There are no changes there. Another point that is important in the new proposals concerns the use of surplus information from communication monitoring, space listening, etc. In the past, it has only been possible if what you have been informed about – other than those you have, for example. have been granted the right to monitor communications for — have also been able to grant the right to monitor communications. Now we're opening up information on serious crime when the police get to know them, in line with the police's duty to solve criminal offences. But there are strict demands for this. It has to be proportionate. This shall not apply to any case. It has been mentioned, for example, in the public debate. speed breaches – if you say something on the phone to someone who is monitoring communication, e.g. about speeding errors, and then you can be judged. It's not that kind of example, it's gotta be a serious crime. The Advocate General shall approve this before communication monitoring can be used, and his own documentary tasks, etc., are to be provided, so that a strict framework is laid for the use of this type of information to solve serious crime. We have different proposals that we might get back to, for example. anonymous witnessing where trafficking in human beings is to be carried out. I would also like to make proposals – one is purely technical, that the Criminal Procedures Act (Section 160a) is repealed. We have taken care of this through changes recently made to the Police Register Act. The proposals were submitted within the time limit.

The distinction between e.g. secret search on one side and phone interception on the other in terms of what can be used as evidence or not is not necessarily easy to explain logically. Police have a general obligation to prevent and solve criminal offences. However, the right considers that the restrictions imposed by the law, by the fact that surplus information may be used as evidence if ‘by the nature of the matter and by the circumstances of the case it would otherwise not be a disproportionate intervention’ and ‘the clarification of the case without the use of the surplus information would be substantially compromised’, are to be interpreted restrictively. It is likely that resource challenges will naturally limit everything, but it is also important that the formal barriers are real formal barriers. The amendments to the Law of the Court of Justice in order to protect third parties must also be clearly assumed from the Storting and have a real limiting effect. As I said at the outset, we expect a rapid evaluation of the impact of the legislative changes – a thorough assessment. And of course, that also applies to this point.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1694	Initially, just a small comment on the question of hidden search. Just let that remark fall before I proceed with the following: I am very pleased that it is a unanimous judicial committee that has given full support to the government's proposal for changes to the Criminal Procedures Act. In particular, I note that the Committee welcomes the amendment of the transparency rules, as emphasised by the Advocate-General as important in the open consultation of the Grand Duchy. We are taking an important step towards strengthening the ability of the police and prosecution authorities to protect sources and informants. In order for them to be willing to provide information to the police, they need to feel secure that their identity is not revealed. In Oslo, there have been cases where the police have had information about major drug networks. But investigations have not been started because witnesses have felt unsafe. We can't have it that way. The prosecution authority should therefore be given a substantial further opportunity to refuse access to criminal documents. A derogation from the right of access is provided for where there may otherwise be a risk that the identity of a person who has provided information to the police will be disclosed. The exemption shall apply to material from any concealed coercive use and not only from communication control, such as today. The rule of anonymous witnessing must be designed in such a way as to provide possible witnesses with predictability and certainty that they will be protected in the form of anonymity if they are presented. It is very unfortunate if the prosecution authority chooses not to testify, for example: police officers working ‘undercover’ because the risk of the witness’s identity becoming known is perceived to be too high. But it is also possible to imagine other witnesses who can feel this very threatening. Access to anonymous witnessing is now extended to war crimes, crimes against humanity and genocide, as well as – I note the Committee’s overall view of it – trafficking in human beings. It also opens the door to protect the identity of witnesses from a number of legal actors, both law-enforcement members, laypeople, defenders and aid lawyers, to prevent the circulation of people with such knowledge from becoming too large, and to limit the risk of leakages. In order to combat crime effectively, it is necessary to extend access to so-called surplus information as evidence. There is information from communication control and space listening on criminal offences other than those intended to be detected by the investigation. In its hearing opinion, Kripos cites an example illustrating the need for a change in law: in 2009, Kripos received information from abroad about a well-known Serbian research environment in Norway. A comprehensive investigation was started using hidden methods. The purpose was to avert the coarse rane of a gold wholesaler. At the same time, the environment planned rough thefts or rough ransacks against individuals. The police had to intervene as they suspected a serious theft from a dwelling where the residents were at home. All the evidence against the suspects was in the results of the hidden investigation and could not therefore be used because of the prohibition on the use of surplus information in criminal proceedings that do not even justify such coercive use. The government therefore proposes to remove this ban. At the same time, we are proposing a number of legislative changes to ensure the legal certainty of the objectives. The use of hidden coercive agents means that a large amount of material, such as sound and image recordings, are collected. For privacy reasons, we therefore propose clearer rules on the deletion of such material that is not used as evidence. We strengthen the protection of the anonymous sources of the press. We also legislate on a general rule of professional secrecy for defenders and aid lawyers.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1827	The proposal and the suspension propose a new Section 8(a) of the passport law. This provision allows the police to use information from the central passport register for more tasks than is provided for in current legislation. In addition to the purposes of the passport law, the police shall be able to retrieve, on the basis of the proposal submitted, information from the passport register for the following use: Working to find a missing person or to identify a dead person or a person it belongs to police duties to help. Furthermore, in order to identify a person who can be or is to be apprehended or arrested in police detention. Furthermore, by preventing or investigating an act that may result in a higher sentence than prison for six months. Furthermore, in work under the Foreign Affairs Act to clarify the identity of a person obliged to provide information on his or her identity, by checking the information to be entered in the Central Register of Criminal Reactions and, finally, when the person to whom the information relates has given an explicit consent based on voluntaryity and information. Following the proposal, the collection of data from the passport register for those purposes shall be possible through so-called direct access. Furthermore, the provision states that information extracted on this basis shall not be stored beyond what is necessary to fulfil the purpose of the processing of the information or to document the examination of the case for which the information has been obtained. In addition, in order to meet the needs arising in practice in order to exchange information in the context of the international police cooperation in which Norway participates, a rule on access to the provision of information from the passport register to foreign police and security services is proposed where this is not considered disproportionate. The Judicial Committee supports the Government's proposal for a new Section 8(a) of the Passport Act and other amendments to the bill that are necessary to adapt the new provision. But I recommend this to the committee's unanimous attitude.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1940	The Committee has examined a proposal from the Left that, since 1993, the UN General Assembly has recommended that countries have a national human rights institution in accordance with the Paris principles. These include recommendations on national institutions, including: to ensure their independence. The Norwegian National Institute for Human Rights (Norwegian Centre for Human Rights) was granted so-called ‘A’ status in 2006 and thus considered fully in line with the Paris principles. However, a report from March 2011 — the so-called Sveaas report — concluded that the link to the University of Oslo has limited the ability of the national institution to fulfil the role of monitoring and driving on human rights issues. In November 2012, the recommendation on downgrading came to B status. The Committee is concerned that Norway remains a driver of human rights issues, both nationally and internationally, and assumes that the government is taking this seriously. The Committee noted that in 2012 the government decided to set up a working group to assess whether the current system should be amended and whether a new national institution should be established. The working group has completed its work and the Committee has taken note of the fact that the report will be sent out at general consultation. The Committee points out that funding needs to be clarified as part of the follow-up to this work. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e1989	Norway is and will continue to be a driver of human rights issues, both nationally and internationally. In Norway, human rights are highly protected. Thanks to the 1999 Law on Human Rights, human rights are given precedence over other laws, where there should be contradictions, and there is a well-developed case law in Norway to this effect. At the same time, it is important that Norway has a national human rights institution in accordance with the international standards set out in the Paris principles of 1993, as we had until last year. Norway, of course, wants to have an institution that can achieve A status in the international network of national institutions, and the government prioritises this work. As the Committee has noted, a cross-ministerial working group has just completed its work of assessing whether a new national institution should be established, what tasks the institution should have, and what form of organisation would be most appropriate. On the basis of the report, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs draws up a consultation document which will be sent to the public in a short time. I hope for broad participation in the consultation round. The final conclusion will be drawn when the recordings from the consultation round are available. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has gained views from a number of human rights actors, including the representatives and civil society. These points of view have been taken into account in the preparation of the recommendations that will be included in the consultation document. In its review of today’s situation, the investigation has concluded that Norway has a well-developed but somewhat fragmented system for monitoring human rights. A new institution is needed to strengthen the coordination and systematisation of human rights work in Norway. A new institution should constitute a professional centre of gravity which can assist both authorities and civil society in human rights matters, while playing an active monitoring and driving role. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has assessed different forms of organisation and also the possibility of adding the task of national institution to existing institutions. The consultation document recommends the establishment of a new independent administrative body subject to the U.D. and the legalisation of the institution's purpose, independence, functioning, tasks, organisation and management in a new law. It is proposed that the institution be given a broad mandate to promote and protect human rights. It is also proposed that a national institution be managed by a board of directors and a director. The Management Board is assumed to provide general guidelines for its work, while the Director shall be responsible for its activities on a daily basis and represent the institution externally. Furthermore, it is recommended that a new institution be associated with an advisory body consisting of representatives of non-governmental organisations, the representatives, other particularly concerned public institutions, the academic environment and specifically concerned professional groups. The power to appoint the Board of Directors of the institution should be shared between the Grand Duchy, the King of the Council of State, the Conseil and the Advisory Forum. Candidate proposals are obtained from a wide range of bodies and institutions. I believe that this model will be fully in line with international standards and will facilitate the establishment of an independent institution, which can reflect the diversity of society. Although the consultation document proposes the establishment of a national institution as a new independent administrative body, it also points out that it would be possible to entrust the task of national institution to an existing body. The consultation document discusses the possibility of adding a new national institution to the Stortinget, either as a new body or by the Civil Ombudsman. The consultation document highlights the advantages and disadvantages of the different options and makes a useful contribution to the discussion on the form in which national institutions should be disclosed. I would like to stress that the government has not taken a decision on the form of organisation that should be used, nor on the financing. The important thing now is to facilitate a broad and good debate before we conclude.

There are at least two principles that can be taken into consideration before deciding on the whole organisation and the proposal procedure and how to integrate the NGO world into Norway in such a process.
