In Zambia, we spent $26 million. to help the authorities increase revenue from the mining industry. The number of people – told me by the authorities – who work with taxation only in the mining sector has increased from 5 to 25. Revenue increased by 1.7 billion. kr, and the amount of the authorities, which increased after education and training during that period, was about SEK 1,7 billion. kr. It only shows how efforts in non-educational areas can help to strengthen the country’s own ability to take on increased education responsibilities. We see that several countries do. Several countries should use larger own tax revenues from basic public services. Many of them understand it very well and already do it. But the aid we gave to Zambia is not on the statistics on education aid, but it has triggered considerable funds. Finally, I fully agree that the aid budget is difficult to reach. Especially in the field where we work according to both thematic and geographical priorities, it's hard to read the numbers out of context. Already in next year's budget, we are taking more action, not only by opening up – as we are doing today – all the databases for public assistance, but also by making representation in the proposition, numbers and text, more transparent and readable. And I'd like to talk to the committee about how we can further make it work better in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2324	At the request of the Labour and Social Committee, the President will propose that the debate be limited to 1 hour and 10 minutes and that speaking time be allocated to the groups as follows: Working Party 25 minutes, Progress Party 15 minutes, Right 10 minutes, Socialist Left Party 5 minutes, Center Party 5 minutes, Christian People's Party 5 minutes and Left 5 minutes. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2333	I record that in the wake of Storting's treatment of the ship's labour law, there has been a debate on gender-neutral concepts, as from now on seafarers in the sense of the law must be found to be workers at sea. Now I think otherwise that sailors will continue to be sailors in most contexts. With the focus we've had on equality in the last few days, however, I hope that the "gravy sailors" will disappear forever and ever with today's decisions in Stortinget. Now, when the Seafarer’s Law is replaced by the Ship’s Labour Act, it is quite undeserved that public attention to the law is linked to cosmetic renewal. The Ship Labour Act is the Law on the Working Environment of seafarers — recognising that working conditions at sea are so special that a law applicable to land cannot automatically be transferred to maritime transport, although the main purpose of the statutory audit that has taken place is to make the environmental law and the ship’s labour law as much as possible. The Ship's Labour Act, which now replaces the Seafarer Act, represents a significant improvement in seafarers' rights. Example of this is a reinforced job protection, extended right to leave and introduction of protection for alerts. One of the changes that may also be noticed is that the age limit, or rather the protection against dismissal due to age, is shifting from 62 to 70 years. This, of course, does not prevent the social partners from agreeing on previous retirement age for health and safety reasons. When the 62-year limit was introduced, it was a worthy end to a long career at sea in order to ensure older seafarers. However, this has gradually become a coercion that also affects those who today have both the desire and the health to be on board after the age of 62. Now, like onshore workers, they can continue to work after that time. I also assume that the age limits of the Ship's Labour Act will continue to be adjusted in line with the age limits of the Labour Environment Act, when applicable. Apart from two areas, there is a relatively broad consensus on the various paragraphs of the Ship Labour Act. However, it is not irrelevant that the Progress Party distinguishes layers with the other parties in view of the employer’s responsibilities for shipping companies and that both the Right and the Advance Party reject the introduction of an administrative fee in the event of a breach of the Ship’s Labour Act. It helps to further doubt the employment of Norwegian seafarers, the recruitment of seafarers and the working conditions of the maritime industry if the voters appoint Erna Solberg and Siv Jensen to rule the country after the September elections. On the one hand, the Right stands, which will limit the net pay system for seafarers according to the so-called model of competence and at the same time introduce NIS on the coast. This in turn will lead to Norwegian shipping companies flagging out, sending Norwegian seafarers on land and replacing them with low-paid Asian crews. On the other hand, the Progress Party, which will extend and enforce the net pay scheme, is opposed to joint and several liability and will introduce national minimum wages on board Norwegian ships. The Progress Party correctly states that many of the measures promoted by the government are contrary to key principles such as free competition and freedom of organisation. Like other parties, the right has recognised that employers' responsibility at sea is now completely different from the one when seafarers were employed in a shipping company that both drove and owned the ship. Today, seafarers are recruited and employed in various forms of crews and rented to the shipping company operating the ship but who do not necessarily own the ship. It is therefore the government, with the support of a broad majority of the Stortinget, establishing a joint and several liability that makes the company responsible for the remuneration and employment of seafarers, even if the actual responsibilities of employers are elsewhere. However, the point is that seafarers’ wage and working conditions are at risk of uncertain outcomes that day – and still against assumption – the Right and Advance Party are negotiating a government basis. On the other hand, it is quite clear that the two batches will reverse today’s decision to allow the Maritime Directorate, as enforcement authority, to issue an administrative fee in the event of a breach of the Ship’s Labour Act. This weakens the ability of maritime administrations to sanction infringements. An overall committee proposes that the employer should at all times inform employees of who is the company at all times and of the obligations under the law of the company. Such information shall be made available in writing. This is of particular importance where joint and several liability is involved. The joint and several liability of the company, in other words, the obligation to ensure that manning undertakings comply with the obligations of the law, has a preventative purpose. Those who abide by the law have nothing to fear. Those who do not do so can be made financially responsible for it. Of course, it is not easy for seafarers to assert their right to a crew company in one country, leased by one operating company in another and a company in a third country. It is therefore necessary to add that other laws may also apply when financial requirements are to be enforced. The Law of the Sea provides for the law of the Sea, i.e.: workers at sea may claim a pledge in a ship in the event of non-payment of remuneration, overtime, holiday pay, food allowance and compensation for unjustified dismissal or dismissal. This is a priority requirement if the Sea Patent Court is applied. The Maritime Organisations, LO and UNIO have called for the Ship's Labour Code to be extended to the field of occupational safety, much the same as the Maritime Code. On the other hand, the joint responsibility of the Ship’s Labour Act at some points is more extensive than in land-based activities, precisely because working conditions at sea are so much special. The government parties and the Christian People's Party believe that international trade in labour, with renting in several stages, makes it necessary to look at such activities in an international context. It is clear that these activities increase the risk of social dumping and contribute to undermining an organised working life. International shipping is no exception in this way — rather the opposite. The Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade has therefore announced that it will follow developments in this area very closely and that it will consider detailed rules for renting. In this context, the majority request that the Ministry also assess the scope of joint and several liability, including the protection of the position. Workers at sea shall, as a general rule, be guaranteed permanent employment, such as on land. However, employers may need to hire temporary workers. As in the Working Environment Act, the employer is now subject to an annual obligation to discuss temporary employment. There is reason to believe that an obligation to discuss in itself will reduce the use of temporary employment. Finally, I would like to state that the obligation to discuss the matter is also imposed upon termination, and that an employee invited to the discussion meeting will need to meet with the trustee. Then it is also natural for collective discussions to be required in larger manning processes. A controversial point in the legislative process has been the right to stand in position while negotiations are ongoing or pending before the courts. Not least the employee organisations have been involved in this. And here there's an improper difference between a ship and any job on land. The majority of the Committee, like the Ministry, is on a compromise basis, i.e.: that an employee must have an unconditional right to stand in a position during negotiations but that the employee does not have the same right during a legal process, unless the right to a claim from the employee determines that it is clearly unfair that the employment is not maintained during the proceedings. I therefore recommend the Committee's position.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2375	The Seamans Act was adopted in 1975. Since then, working life has been changing, including at sea. The result of these developments has led to a selection that has looked at harmonisation of the Law on Seafarers and the Law on Working Environment, which has now become the Shipwork Act. The Shipwork Act takes into account much of the same rules as the Labour Environment Act. The party of progress believes it is important to have good and safe working conditions, including for those working at sea, and has supported several of the changes. Nevertheless, the Progress Party has pointed out that many of the measures promoted by the government are against central and superior principles such as free competition and freedom of organisation. These schemes also represent a public intervention in free contract law and the responsibility of the parties for wage formation. In particular, this measure applies to the public sector. It is therefore necessary to clarify that the Progress Party has an own proposal to provide a national minimum wage as an alternative to public administration and as a response to undesirable working conditions in the Norwegian labour market. The Shipwork Act has also been given its own ‘alert paragraph’. The progress party is pleased that a notification paragraph will now be inserted in the new law. When the government in June 2006 proposed changes to the Working Environment Act in order to ensure that people who warn about illegal conditions in the labour market would be better protected. The authority's rationale for introducing the alert paragraph was that a more alert was requested. Warnings seem positive for democracy, society and the enterprises. Nevertheless, we see that today's alert paragraph works mainly against its purpose, both as a result of design and as a result of the lack of compliance with the intentions behind it. Still, there are alerts who have poor protection, despite the fact that the government has said the opposite. The point of progress is concerned that there are many conditions that are not notified, because the stress of standing up actually becomes too high, and that the protection against the alert is not affected. The Progress Party therefore proposes in 2011 to set up a selection to evaluate how the alert paragraph works. Not so much has happened in this way, and alerts continue to experience that it is a major inconvenience to say whether or not there are critical issues in the world of work. We have daily contact with the notifyers who actually want to notify us, but who do not dare. The Progress Party has called for a net pay regime here and has proposed this in the context of the revised national budget this year. We have also recorded that the organisations of seafarers believe that the maritime industry needs a net pay system. In the government’s 2013 draft government budget, it was proposed to set of 1 600 million. kr for employment measures for Norwegian seafarers. This amount was also proposed in the State budget for 2012. Taking into account the annual wage and price growth in society, this represents a real decline in the wages of the employment of seafarers. The government said in the budget proposal that the reason for this is that you want to limit the growth within this area. The reimbursement system is very important for the fleets of this debt, all the time they operate in an international market where many of the countries with which we compete have much better arrangements than what we have in Norway. While one from Norway has chosen a reimbursement scheme limited to NOK 198 000, several of the countries with which we compete have chosen a model with a net pay scheme. The Progress Party now believes that a work must be undertaken to legislate a fully-fledged net pay system for seafarers, so that one is more competitive towards the countries with which we compete and at the same time gives the industry greater success. That's why we think it should work internationally to eliminate this form of nutritional subsidiar. Until agreement can be reached in this area, Norway should also have competitive arrangements for the employment of Norwegian seafarers. The Ship Labour Act also addresses the use of contracted labour and temporary additions. The party of progress has become the debt of both the government and the LO to ruin working life, because we want more employees into ordinary work. The progress bar has always indicated that the main addition rule should be fixed and the temporary addition rule should be as it is today, four years. However, we have also said that we want there to be room to use temporary inclusion in order to postpone groups to be able to enter the labour market, so that they also get work experience and build their own CV. If this is to ruin the working life, I thought those who claim it should ask all those who are on the outside, who do not feel that way. It must be better to be paid by an employer outside than within Nav. In maritime transport, of course, there are different needs for temporary additions. In maritime transport, employers need to be able to adapt the workforce in the event of fluctuations in labour demand. In particular: fishing is the very seasonal nature of the activity itself, and in the domestic fleet there are major seasonal variations in the need for certain categories of workers. Seafarers also become pensioners, and the Seafarer Act gives the right to denounce a worker with age as a foundation in the blink of an eye who is 62 years old. The Ship's Labour Act opens up the possibility that the protection of the position will remain in force until the age of 70. The Progress Party supports this change and believes that age alone is not the basis for giving up a worker. Therefore, the Progress Party has previously put forward a proposal to allow workers to work at the age of 75. The general rule in the pension scheme for seafarers is that seafarers who have accumulated at least 150 months of seagoing service are entitled to a seafarer’s pension from 60 years of age or 62 years of age if they are still at work. When the age limit for seafarers increases from 62 to 70 years, this will have consequences for the pension scheme. The change to raise the age limit for seafarers does not require a review of the pension rate for seafarers, based on the increase in age and the impact this will have on the retirement of seafarers. The Party of Progress has therefore proposed such a review in order to address the impact of the change. Finally, I take up the proposals of the Progress Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2475	The Ship Labour Act, the Maritime Working Environment Act, is a very important law for the Central Coastal Party. In this short article, I will focus solely on job protection. The coast of Norway has good conditions for shipping. The framework conditions for shipping are linked to the yard and the GIEK scheme, the compensation of seafarers and zero in company tax for shipping companies. It's about billions. The system has been successful, it has created optimism — it is a social contract with rights and obligations. What we're talking about now is the conditions of the seamen – relationships that go on between people, between people. What is happening in the labour market is crucial for the general quality of life. It's important for us to fight for Cohesion Norway, that's why this law is important. What's going on? Well, functions are split between company and formal employers, and the danger of pulverising employer responsibility is present. If the employer’s responsibility is unclear, several actors may turn away from normal obligations, whether in relation to laws, contracts or day-to-day tasks. At international speed, the division of employer tasks is a practical rule. There are three companies: ship-ownership companies, commercial companies and crew companies or crew companies. Until now, domestic shipping has been employed by the commercial shipping company. But progress is in the direction of splitting. A new example is the high-speed route, with Trygve Hegnar as a major owner, which has otherwise secured large government appropriations to the company, where one is in favour of employing its own companies outside the traditional operating company. The problem is that the manning company usually does not have commercial values, economics or decision-making power in terms of operational assessments, which is essential for the ship as a job. A formal employer can deal little with normal responsibilities, job protection and other matters that are important for the work situation on board. The government proposes joint responsibility for the shipping companies. That's good. But it is a limited joint and several liability, which does not guarantee the seafarers well enough. After the proposal, the joint and several liability of the company shall end with wages, holiday pay and any other limited financial requirements. This means that other claims against the employer arising from addition contracts or other legal grounds cannot be enforced. Claims for compensation in connection with, for example, termination, discrimination and warnings cannot therefore be required by the shipping company, despite particular features of shipping, such as the absence of an employer’s right of instruction in the workplace. Manning companies are only responsible for the formal responsibilities of the employer. In addition, the reason for the termination will often be due to the company’s relationship with the operator, not with the manning company. It is gratifying that an overall committee considers that the use of manning companies, intermediary companies and crew companies of a different nature contributes to the dilution of employers' responsibilities. Therefore, it is a minimum requirement that the employee should know who is the shipping company at any time. In particular, it is important to make the applicable joint and several liability, and therefore the obligations of the company under sections 2 to 4. Therefore, the second sentence of the second paragraph of Section 2-2 of the new law states that the employer has a general obligation to inform the employee of who is the company at any time. There's an overall committee that makes the law better on this point, and that's good. Seafarers’ organisations are called upon to extend joint and several liability in addition to that contained in the government’s proposal for a new ship’s labour law. The position of seafarers' organisations is new to the third paragraph of § 2-4: «The shipowner shall be jointly and severally liable to the employer for the financial claims of the employee who has worked on the ship under the law or under the law of the law or under the terms of the contract of employment.» The centre party agrees with this, but it has not been possible to have a majority in favour of this improvement in the law. We only got the improvement in the third paragraph of Section 2-2, which was agreed by an overall committee. However, it is important to point out that all parties, with the exception of the Right Party and the Advance Party, say that when assessing rental rules, a Ministry is also asking to assess the scope of joint and several liability, including job protection. It's essential that it happens. We need solid companies that think in the long term. That's what the shipping tax is built on: the social contract with employees and the Norwegian flag, with rights and obligations. It is therefore essential that the shipping companies show that they are following this. It is crucial for the favourable framework conditions under which shipping companies base in Norway.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2508	It is very gratifying that today workers on ships are being protected by the new Ship Labour Act. Work on ships has its own peculiarities which mean that the law should not, or may, be entirely in line with the Working Environment Act, but the law should nevertheless provide a good and adequate protection for workers on ships. The Christian People's Party has essentially supported the government's proposal. The background is that account has been taken of the specific circumstances which are part of the working situation of shipboard workers. The new law contributes to the promotion of job protection for these workers, while ensuring the necessary flexibility for shipping and for the fishing industry. In order to maintain and create jobs in the shipping and fishing industries, it is essential that the legislation takes into account both flexibility on the employer’s side and sufficient protection for the worker in order to ensure predictability. In the case of the right to work on termination, the law must take account of the fact that the workplace is a 24-hour society. It is therefore essential that this right, as a starting point, does not extend beyond the duration of the negotiations, and not throughout the legal process, as the provisions of the Labour Environment Act state. Everyone's safety must be taken into account. The Christian People's Party is very concerned that working life can be combined with family life. It is therefore very important that an extension of workers' rights to leave is now adopted. A prerequisite for parents to be able to work at sea is that they, among other things: shall be entitled to freedom in the event of a child’s disease and an extended right to maternity leave. If the protection of seafarers is to be effectively strengthened, it depends on the existence of clear rules on responsibility positioning. It is therefore positive that the company's joint and several liability is now clarified by the fact that they are responsible for ensuring that the employer complies with their obligations. Shipowners will be protected by the law adopted today, which means that seafarers are granted rights equivalent to those of other workers under the Working Environment Act. That is Christian People's Party very pleased. The combination of worker protection rules and flexibility for employers is central to achieving a sustainable working life at sea. But in respect of generations of Norwegian sailors, the red button will probably be somewhat more tempting than usual when we're going to vote over the last part of the law decision this time.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2534	The draft Ship Labour Act is intended to replace the current Seafarer Act of 1975. Although the Seafarer Act has been subject to some changes since its adoption, no complete revision of the protection of seafarers’ employment law has been carried out since then. At the same time, the protection of workers on land under labour law has been strengthened during this period, in particular by the adoption of new labour law in 2005. A main consideration in the design of the Ship Labour Act has therefore been to look at the rights of other workers under the Working Environment Act and to bring up as far as possible the rights of seafarers at the same level. At the same time, there are specific issues that arise from working at sea, which both indicate that labour at sea should continue to be regulated by its own law, and that legislation should contain sufficient flexibility and adaptations to the needs of shipping and fishing fleets. It is also important to take into account that significant parts of the Norwegian fleet are exposed to competition, primarily foreign shipping and offshore. If the regulation of operations under the Norwegian flag becomes significantly different from operations under the foreign flag, Norwegian flag may be removed – with the loss of Norwegian jobs. The draft law presented by the government involves both an improvement in job protection and an extension of rights which, overall, provides a significant strengthening of the position of seafarers. At the same time, the legislative proposal substantially maintains the competitiveness of Norwegian ships engaged on international voyages. I will now briefly point to the main features of the new law. The development of job protection has been at a slower pace than for other workers. This is where the draft law will give you more improvements. Among other things, it is proposed to strengthen the right of priority, including the right of priority for part-time employees, and to propose an obligation to inform about vacancies in the establishment. The draft law proposes to continue today's general rule of permanent employment. However, it is proposed to introduce an obligation to discuss the use of temporary employment and that employees who have been employed for at least four years are entitled to permanent employment. Seafarers today have better rights than other workers in the event of incapacity for work due to sickness or injury. Shipowners have a similar financial burden in such situations as employers on land. It is proposed to maintain this special right for seafarers, but at the same time limit the employer's pay obligation to one year from disability. Furthermore, a right is proposed for seafarers to continue in the position in case of dispute as to whether a termination is illegal. The Seafarer Act does not contain rules on the protection of alerts. Although I can see that the special considerations that apply to the fact that the ship is a 24-hour society, it is in my opinion right to introduce equivalent external warning provisions to those in the Working Environment Act. The draft law also modernises and clarifies the procedural rules in accordance with the Labour Environment Act, with its own provisions on the right to require negotiations, time limits, extension and refresher, and on the legal handling of disputes concerning employment in Norway. The aim of further work has made it necessary to assess whether the 62-year limit of the Seafarer Act should be raised. Judicial practices also show that statutory age limits must be within the scope of exemption from the age limit ban under the EU Framework Directive. In my view, the general age limit should be raised to 70 years, while explicitly opening up lower limits to other grounds within the framework of the EU anti-discrimination ban. Another important strengthening of the rights in the law relates to the rights of leave. Right to leave is an important asset in a modern working life. Allowances are particularly important in order to adapt participation in working life to family and care obligations, and they have seen a gender equality page. Liability has been one of the most central, and at the same time the most difficult, issues in the design of the law. The starting point of the draft law – as under the current Maritime and Environmental Protection Act – is that it is the employer who is primarily responsible for ensuring that the requirements of the law are met by the employee. At the same time, it is the case in shipping that the employer will not always be the same as the driver of the ship. Both the international obligations and the fact that the operator is responsible for the significant risk factors on the ship and actually exercises control and control of the workers on board indicate that responsibility must also be placed with the operator of the shipowner. Firstly, an obligation is proposed for the company to ensure that the employer complies with his obligations under the law and the employment contract. Secondly, it is proposed to render the company jointly and severally liable with the employer for financial rights arising from the Act, Chapter 4. Solidarity must be seen in the context of labour hiring. The draft law does not propose any restrictions on access to rental, nor has an overall sample been proposed. From the point of view of the sample, it was concluded that the organisation of activities by shipping means that the introduction of such rules could have significant consequences. We must monitor developments in this area and assess the implementation of such provisions if the situation so warrants. The adoption of the Ship Labour Act will be a milestone for thousands of seafarers. It represents the largest reform of working and living conditions at sea of almost 40 years.

Reduced ice deployment is in the process of making shipping routes along the Arctic Ocean commercially interesting. One solution may be that medicinal products that have completed the study programme are not included in the exchange list/step price before six months after the expiry of the patent. In addition, I would like to point out that, in the context of the Right’s proposal for a long-term strategy for biomedicine, Document 8:48 S for 2010–2011, it was proposed that a case be brought before the Stortinget on the legal status of the exchange of medicinal products – biological medicinal products in particular – where, among other things, the dossier. explain how the government considers the legal situation to be and should be, in the future.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2830	Report. St. 28, Unique Ideas, High Values – on Intangible Values and Rights – were presented to the Stortinget on April 12, 2013. Here the government presents a long-term policy to contribute to greater strategic, conscious and profitable use of intangible values and rights in Norwegian business and research. By providing such a good framework for increasing innovation, the Communication shall contribute to the objectives of industrial policy for the maximum overall value creation in the Norwegian economy and work for all. International economic conditions and framework conditions change rapidly, in which case the management of intangible assets and rights must also be changed. In particular, the number of intellectual property rights registered internationally is increasing. In emerging economies, businesses are increasingly securing such rights. International trade and cooperation also often involve the management of intellectual property rights. Developments increase the need for competence and awareness of the management of intellectual property rights as a strategic tool in the development of Norwegian business. It also increases the need for up-to-date legislation adapted to the economic reality of business. The future competitiveness of Norway depends on resilient businesses and workers putting into service and building more knowledge and technology into products. This includes, inter alia: that establishments must have an awareness and competence of intangible values. When intangible values become economically important, they also have a greater impact on industrial policy. Analysis of the communication shows that very many in Norway do not protect their innovations, that piracy is widespread also in Norway and affects Norwegian businesses, that the competence and awareness of the subject area is likely to be too low in parts of Norwegian business, in the academia, in health businesses, in the instrumentation, in public authorities and most of the people. Lack of competence and awareness may have serious consequences. This could lead to lower competitiveness and loss of earnings for businesses, to their innovations being copied by competitors, to their responsibility for interference with the rights of others, or to their being perceived as less attractive partners. Therefore, the challenge of competence needs to be taken seriously, and therefore it is a central element of the Communication. We've had a lot of work on the message to get recordings from many teams. I believe that the Communication has a good mix of entirely concrete, immediate and longer-term actions within a broad range of areas of action. Important instruments include improved training provision, further development of the Patenary Board, regulatory improvement, combating piracy and counterfeiting, and enhanced knowledge base. We are pleased that the main features of the message have received good support in Stortinget, and we are happy to work on the follow-up of an important work.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e2977	Active enterprise policy is essential to create values and jobs across the country. Norway is benefiting greatly from a strong profitable oil industry, which allows it to strengthen the state budget every year. At the same time, large parts of industry compete in an international market. Conditions in Norway, which are designed to be completely different from those of foreign operators, may impair the competitiveness of enterprises and may result in the closure or displacement of operations by newly established capacity in better-performing countries. There are a number of areas where Norwegian special schemes and rules weaken the competitiveness of Norwegian industry. The extraordinary wealth tax is an example of that. As a result of the taxes on wealth, many enterprises make more profits to owners in order to finance owners' property taxes. Especially is the family-owned company with spread ownership. In addition, it dampens the appetite for investment in production equipment and favours investment in real estate. It is an additional tax on Norwegian capital compared to capital owned by the rest of the world. Most countries Norway usually compares with Norway have abolished the wealth tax. In OECD countries, only France, Switzerland, Spain, Iceland and Norway have taxes on wealth. Of these, Norway has the weakest protection mechanisms for industrial wealth, or so-called working capital. An exemption from property taxes on working capital will remove the tax discrimination that Norwegian private owners have relatively compared to public owners and foreign owners today. It will stimulate national private ownership. It will thus remove the competitive disadvantage that the Norwegian capital market has for domestic investors. Industry and other industries need stable framework conditions to ensure further development. In particular, in the case of initial investment. Any uncertainty contributes to the risk that lowers the return on initial investment, production and research. In Norway, there is too much tendency for framework conditions to change from year to year. Enterprises are exposed to the removal of research funds overnight, the re-interpretation of tax rules such as SkattenFUNN, new taxes and changed plans for the environment and climate. This is unfortunate and contributes to lower investment and less profitable business. During the 1990s, there were few changes in the framework conditions for business in Norway. It contributed to a strong recovery in investment and employment in industry and other value added. Over the last 15 years, there have been a number of changes in industrial framework conditions from year to year. For example: Sweden introduced a Green Electricity Certificate for Renewable Power Development in 2003, Norway spent the years until 2012 on a number of different schemes, before Norway finally joined the Swedish electricity certificate market in 2012. The result has been a significant increase in Swedish investment in wind power from 2005, while Norwegian investment has been very low, mainly due to uncertainty about the framework conditions from year to year. I believe this shows, among other things: the need for better and more stable and predictable framework conditions. I hereby promote the proposals of the Christian People's Party as they proceed from the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e3172	The representative proposal by Harald T. Nesvik, Per Roar Bredvold, Torgeir Tjäldal and Bård Hoksrud, on the framework conditions for Norwegian forestry and woodworking industries, is a highly topical issue in the field of industrial policy. The Norwegian timber industry is highly competitive – and labors. The Swedish company Södra Cell is now liquidating its ownership at its plant in Hurum Municipality due to a lack of profitability. A liquidation will have a direct impact on 300 employees, but according to the Norwegian Forest Association, almost 6 000 jobs can be affected. The South Cell case is not a unique example, but shows the good challenges for the Norwegian woodworking industry. Managing Director of Norwegian Forest states: ‘Serious rules are daunting us for 200 million a year.’ There is mutual dependence in Norwegian forest industries, and a weakened wood processing industry may threaten the entire industry. There is good reason for concern that the hourly earnings in Norwegian industry today are about 70 pst. higher than our trading partners, and that we have a lower level of innovation. The annual growth of productivity under red-green control has suffered a dramatic decline. Our competitiveness is greatly reduced. The proposal points out that the peculiar wealth tax results in companies making dividends to owners in order to finance the owners' wealth tax, which reduces their ability to invest and their soundness. There has been a hidden increase in taxes by reducing depreciation rates, and government parties have voted down proposals to improve the depreciation rules. The government also sees that action is needed to save the Norwegian forest industry. On 29 April 2013, the so-called forest package was presented. There's good reason to ask yourself the question of why this first came at the tame of the government's second growing period. Stortingsretten Per Olaf Lundtigen stated to the NRK on 5 April: ‘We have failed the forestry industry’. The heritage of the Stoltenberg government is a forest industry under critical size. Under the red-green government, the number of jobs in the forest industry has been reduced by approx. 40 pst. In the same period, employment — within the same industry — has remained stable in Sweden. There is room for a Norwegian forest industry, but it requires good political action. The Progress Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party propose that a separate forest report be submitted and that a concrete long-term objective be put in place for modular train of up to 25 metres and a total weight of up to 60 tonnes to be used on the main road network where it is dimensioned for such transport. The Progress Party and the Right propose to abolish the requirement of residence for forest property, open to the share company model as a form of operation in forestry, and we ask the Government to ensure that the concept of ‘non-interventional natural areas’ is no longer given importance in dealing with land matters in government administrations. I take up the proposals of the Advance Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e3244	Economical and active forestry is a vital prerequisite for maintaining and further developing as extensive a wood-based industry in Norway as possible. The ownership laws of forestry and agriculture shall facilitate economic development, activity and future in Building Norway. Housing is a key instrument of rural and agricultural policy and contributes to maintaining rural settlements. The winding-up of the housing obligation will result in capital investors buying up farm and other district properties, and much of the year will be destroyed by rural communities. The issue of compulsory residence and compulsory concession is an example of the dividing lines that are so clear in Norwegian agricultural and district policies. The right-hand side – like representatives of the financial elite – is more concerned with providing a rich opportunity to own more recreational properties in District Norway than ensuring housing and industrial activity in the districts. With regard to the proposal to open up public limited liability companies as a form of forestry, we in the Centerpartie want agricultural property to be owned to the greatest extent possible by persons who are self-employed and who live on the property. However, in cases where flexible solutions on the part of owners give advantages that traditional forms of ownership do not have, the law already facilitates this. In the revised national budget for 2013, the Government has proposed a package of measures of 750 million. kr, which contains measures that will help the forest industry both in the short and long term, which show that the government is taking the challenges facing the industry seriously. We in the Center Party are also very pleased that the business report announces a separate strategy for the forest industry. SKOG22 shall be broadly composed of persons from the whole value chain. The strategy shall provide the basis for a strong research and development effort and demonstration activity – this in order to realise a significant value creation potential in the wood as a resource for the transition from oil-based to bio-based products. Forestry is one of the few complete value chains in Norway. It covers forestry and services related to forestry, timber, timber and wood processing industries. The industry means a lot to value creation and employment in districts. As is well known, timber can be used for a number of modern products, and therefore research and development are the keywords of the government’s new initiative in the Norwegian forest industry. Cost-effective transport is essential for the forest industry. The proposed regulatory changes to increase the total weight of timber transport to 60 tonnes have now been consulted. The government has previously opened for increased length, from 22 metres to 24 metres, on a wooden wagon train. These measures will contribute to the reduction of industry costs and have been received very well.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e3263	Forestry and forest industries have worn out in recent years, and the number of people employed in the forest industry has been reduced by about 40 pst. under this Government. The current situation of the forest industry in Norway is challenging. Developments in the production of cellulose, wood pulp, paper, cardboard, plates, etc. are so dramatically negative that large parts of the industrial demand for forest can be lost in parts of the country. There are a number of measures that could significantly improve the situation of forestry, both in terms of infrastructure, taxes and taxes, and the use of funds within the framework of the code of action. It is good that the government now looks at the weight and length limitation of vehicle trains. A change to 25 metres and 60 tonnes will make a large difference for timber, wood and tile transport, and provide an annual savings of approximately 50,000 loads. Unfortunately, however, changes in the length and weight limits, the development of forest roads, or the restoration of highways will not have much to do without improving the county roads. In large parts of the country there is a poorly developed and maintained county road network. It even recognises the government itself on page 115 of the National Transport Plan, which states: ‘A significant decline has occurred over time on the county road network.’ The state road service has documented that it costs about 75 billion. to remove the decay of the county roads. In the context of the national transport plan, the government has proposed an increase of 10 billion. in framework transfers to the counties, which will not be due for more than five years — during the last part of the planning period. At the same time, the system of interest compensation for transport operations is eliminated. The Christian People's Party believes that this will not be enough to address the very precarious needs for re-establishment of the county road network. Therefore, we will continue the interest-rate compensation scheme so that the counties are still assisted in the development and improvement of the county road network in order to correct bottlenecks and establish adequate carrying capacity on the roads. This is crucial for the transport of timber in the parts of the country from which I come. Taxes and taxes also weaken the competitiveness of Norwegian forestry. The Christian People's Party wants to remove the wealth tax on working capital and start a review of the depreciation rates. We will also see whether there is a need for a further reduction in corporate tax in addition to the government presented in the revised national budget, and for the inheritance tax to be abolished. In addition, we will maintain the average equation for forestry in order to strengthen long-term production. Finally, I would like to mention the rule of action. It is alarming that the government is disregarding the premise for the introduction of the rule of action. It was a prerequisite that the funds from our oil wealth were to be used for research, education, infrastructure or business-oriented tax relief. That hasn't happened. Resources for industrial research are falling, the infrastructure has a significant lag, and the Norwegian level of taxation is not competitive. The Christian People's Party will support the proposals of the Progress Party, both in terms of changes in the length and gravity limits of vehicle trains, and the proposal for a separate report on forest.

Through lending to owner-occupiers and loans to small and large enterprises, banks support development, innovation and growth both for individuals and businesses across the country. Increased capital requirements in combination with low interest rates and increasing life expectancy, which increase the value of company liabilities, present a not insignificant challenge in the future. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e4002	I want to use the opportunity to include two things in my post. One is strict capital requirements. The same applies to the House Bank, which was also the representative of Syversen. Municipalities report an increase in the number of applications and granted start-up loans through the Husbank. Therefore, the increased requirement for equity needs to be reviewed and replaced by other instruments, such as requirements for loan security and better arrangements for saving for owner-occupied dwellings through residential savings for young people. The left considers it reasonable to impose an indicative equity requirement of 10 pst. in the case of the purchase of a dwelling, but will allow banks to exercise discretion in loan commitments, inter alia. based on prospects for future income. The second thing I want to address is access to loans for small and medium-sized enterprises. These holdings constitute the backbone of Norwegian business. Over 99 pst. by enterprises in Norway have 100 employees or fewer. One of the main challenges facing business is access to capital, including: through loans from the banks. As a result of new and stricter capital requirements, bank lending to business is now falling. The long-term consequences can be considerable if this is a continuing development. As part of the EU regulation on new capital requirements – to which Norway is obliged to comply – a provision has been introduced to encourage banks to grant credit to small and medium-sized enterprises. These are borrowers who would be particularly likely to be affected when, as a result of stricter capital requirements, banks tighten their lending practices. It is therefore very important that Norway have the same understanding of the EU Regulation on new capital requirements, so that small and medium-sized enterprises in Norway are treated in the same way as the countries around us as regards the possibility of obtaining loans and access to capital within the framework of a common regulatory framework. Unfortunately, both the Minister of Finance and the Government parties are reluctant about this point. The importance of small and medium-sized enterprises is often referred to by the government parties in this room. But this does not reflect – as the Left sees it – in this case.

There is still considerable uncertainty internationally, and we also see that this can impact on the Norwegian economy. For this reason, there is a wide range of work both in Norway and internationally to develop new regulation and supervision and to strengthen the soundness of the financial industry. For Norway: when the economy is doing well, when there are good results in the financial industry, when there are opportunities, as I see it now, we have to build reserves. Often it is, and it is also a lesson from history, that it is in good times that one can make mistakes and lose a little bit of sight – which means that one has to pay dearly later, as we said instead. There are some challenges in the future, both in Norway and internationally, in financial industries. One is that we now have very low interest rates, both internationally and in Norway, and the effect of low interest rates over time, as it appears on the day it becomes more normalized, is an important issue to focus on. We see that there has been a significant flow of liquidity both in European and US banks. What happens to the day when it is necessary to withdraw any of this? On a slightly smaller scale, we have the fact that one in several countries has a long margin on loans combined with low interest rates. What happens on the day of the repayment, and higher interest rates enter, which they will do at one time or another? There are many things to consider within this industry. Solid banks and a solid financial industry should also be able to contribute to confidence – in the individual, but not least in the market, in order to give Norwegian banks the best possible deposit conditions. I want to end with a quote from the outgoing governor of England, Mervyn King, who said, "Banks are global in life and local in death." It is the responsibility of the individual bank and the responsibility of each country to ensure that we have solid banks, because that day something happens, that crises must be handled locally, there and then. In other words, we must be prepared.

Over the last year, the representativeness has followed up on how the bank has built up the property portfolio in the SPU and has in this context addressed an independent assessment of the governance and control framework for property investments. This is necessary all the time property investment is new for the management of the SPU. The Committee therefore also welcomes the fact that this area has been followed up by its representative during the last year in particular. The Governing Council of the Bank of Norway shall direct the carrying on and advisory activities of the Bank, while its representation shall exercise supervision and control. The representative office has its own secretariat and supervises the operation of the bank and the rules governing the operation of the bank. The Agency shall establish the accounts of the Bank of Norway and adopt a budget. Several changes have been made in recent years in the area of representation, not least through the above-mentioned clarification made in the 2009 Central Bank Act on Supervisory and Supervisory Responsibilities, and by the 2010 representation reporting directly to the Stortinget. The Committee considers that it is important to assess on an ongoing basis how the supervision of the Bank's activities can be further developed, including the role of its representation. It is important that the good development that has been over the last few years continues. The prioritisation of further supervisory work facilitates this, inter alia: through continued high priority in areas such as risk management, internal control, risk mitigation measures and compliance with laws and regulations. The Committee takes the view that the Governing Council has had satisfactory governance and control with the Bank of Norway in 2012. With this I recommend the setting of the committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e4320	The financial statements are the last line item of the budgetary wheel, where we finalise the balance sheet for the previous year. The status of 2012 tells us that Norway went well in the year we now have behind us. The oil-corrected deficit was 19 billion. kr lower than in the aged budget adopted late in the autumn of 2011. There are two main reasons for that. Firstly, expenditure was less than intended. More than half of the minor consumption is caused by lower costs than originally assumed. In addition, revenue has increased. Tax revenue was totaled at 7 billion. kr higher than that assumed. So someone would say that 19 billion. kr is a fairly healthy number, a significant deviation. To that, the government points out, and I agree, that there will always be some uncertainty or uncertainty about figures to be provided in the budget, or other macroeconomic figures, and the changes in 2012 do not differ from those that have been normal deviations. Safe economic governance is, in my opinion, about creating budgets based on the best possible professional estimate in a very uncertain economy and the world. Then we're always going to use the best models we have to calculate estimates that point forward in economic development. It is therefore good that the Ministry of Finance, through both attention and a separate sample, addresses exactly what we are all concerned with: ensuring the best possible professional basis for dealing with the preparation of the annual budgets. I'd like to add that it's now become the rule that when you're bombing, you're fortunately bombing to a great extent by getting more revenue and less expenditure. It's a much better world to be in than where how many other countries have been, where one has bombed the other way, and the revenue has been lower and the cost is higher. Then countries often have much bigger problems. If we move on to our money on the book, we see that the book value of the Staten pension fund abroad at the end of 2012 is 3,825 billion. kr. It's over 500 billion. kr more than at the end of 2011. The wealth we have on the book, which we pick up from the base, is good, but it also requires that we take responsibility, that we manage the values well. We have to make sure that the extra resources we are now converting to a monetary value are not phased into the economy at such a rate that the economy is going overboard. Such a fortune does not come of itself and does not remain great if we do not take responsibility in this hall. With this I recommend the setting of the government accounts.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e4386	Today, the law of law in this city has sent an appeal on environmental limits in return because the basis of the law was not present, and what this matter is about is that this bill is precisely designed to ensure the legal basis for the possible use of environmental shipping limits. There has been an alarming slow progress in the process of clarifying the basis on which we now have the environmental limit. It has been unclear for several years whether the police have the right to enforce the measure, and it has been almost two years since the Right and Christian People's Party proposed that the government should clarify precisely this legal basis for the environmental border in Oslo, so that the action could be continued without delay. The aim of the proposal is to clarify that the environment, including the protection of public health, is a general consideration of the Road Traffic Act and that exceeding speed limits established for environmental reasons should follow the normal reaction system for speed limit infringements. Road wear and re-suspension are a major source of particulate matter. After constant exceedances of the limit value of the pollution regulation for particulate matter, an environmental test project was launched in 2004 at Riksvej 4 in Oslo, resulting in a reduction of the speed limit on the route from 80 km/h to 60 km/h in the peak season to reduce the suspended particulate matter. There have been good results from this experiment, and this has led to the introduction of the environmental limit on Ring 3 in 2006 and the Framnes-Lysaker section on E18 in 2008. There are many – too many – who are plagued by air and noise pollution, and the Norwegian Association against Noise and the Asthma and Allergy Association also point out the ongoing health plagues that are being inflicted on people by road traffic. The environmental limit can be a useful measure to improve air quality. For the right, it is important to point out that there are local governance powers that should have the opportunity to have the widest possible toolbox in order to ensure a good urban environment. It is also important for the Right to stress that local authorities must decide whether to introduce environmental limits within the municipality border. In this respect, we believe that environmental limits must also be an instrument that is available to local politicians, and which can provide the best assessment, but also the best effect, for having a good urban environment, but also for improving air quality.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-13.ana.ud1271e4399	There is a short time left for the Storting to deal with the major national transport plan notification for the next decade. It is clear to the government that transport policy should contribute to reducing the environmental impact of transport and also contribute to meeting national health and environmental objectives. The Communication also states that, in order to achieve the national objectives for clean air and less noise, the government will implement measures for the most vulnerable areas and will also continue to work to set nightlife targets. It is also clarified that the environment is one of the elements that can be taken into account when setting speed limits. The proposal under consideration today clarifies the legal framework and the response legislation for environmental limits, and it clarifies precisely that the environment, including noise, can be one consideration in the speed limitation of road and rail authorities. Furthermore, it is also clarified that exceedances of a speed limit fixed in full or in part for environmental reasons shall follow the usual reaction code for speed limits. It should be recognised that there has been a long process around this amendment, but the change currently under consideration will form the legal framework for environmental limits, for example. to reduce particulate matter or noise, either for the restoration of the three environmental limits in Oslo or for the establishment of new environmental limits elsewhere in the country. The legislative proposal under consideration today has been subject to general consultation, and the final proposal has received broad support from the hearing bodies, among others. by the National Attorney, the police and road, health and environmental authorities. I have noted the comments made by the majority of the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection that it is a great advantage for local authorities to have the widest possible toolbox in order to improve the urban environment, and that it must be up to local authorities to decide whether environmental maritime borders should be introduced within the municipal border. However, when the proposal is made in relation to the matter and the possibility of ordinary treatment is very limited at this time of the year, the President, since the matter we are dealing with, including the criminal law, will propose that the proposal be dealt with in accordance with the second paragraph of Section 38 of the Storting Rules of Procedure. Point (d) of that provision shall mean that a decision may be taken without delay if the President or one fifth of the representatives present do not oppose it. On this basis, the President proposes that the proposal by the Progressive Party be put to the vote. No objections have been raised against it – and it is deemed to have been adopted. Proposal No. 1, from the Progress Party, reads: «Agreement to the Law amending the Law of 22 May 1902 No 10 General Civil Punishment I I Law 22 May 1902 No. Section 67 is replaced by the following: [[renewal of limitation periods]] § 67 The limitation period for limitation is 10 years when the highest statutory penalty is imprisonment up to 6 years and 30 years when the highest statutory penalty is imprisonment for more than 6 years. Criminal offences where the highest statutory penalty is 21 or more years old are not outdated. The same applies to criminal offences under Sections 193, 194, 195, 196, 197 and 198. For the purposes of calculating the time limit, booklets are considered to be similar to prisons. The imposition of fines or loss of rights in addition to other penalties is not relevant in the calculation of the time limit. If a person has committed several offences in the same act, which were to expire at different times after the first subparagraph, the maximum limitation period applies to all infringements. The time limit for the limitation of criminal liability of undertakings shall be calculated on the basis of the penalties applicable to individuals in the criminal offence. II The amendments to Section 67 of the Criminal Code also apply to criminal offences committed before the adoption of the law here. III The law shall enter into force immediately.’ The right, Christian People's Party and the left have announced that they support the proposal. The President expects the Labour Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Central Party to vote against.
